proof.
∎

See
5

Proof

We prove the
c
-best-from-below case only. The other case can be proven analogously.
First, we show that each model
\nu
of
\varphi(\vec{x},c^{\prime})
is also a model of

\exists y(\varphi(\vec{x},y)\land y\mathrel{\equiv_{m}}k)
. Consider
\nu^{\prime}=\nu\cup\{y\mapsto c^{\prime}\}
.
Since
c^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{m}}k
, we have that
\nu^{\prime}
is a model of
\varphi(\vec{x},y)\land y\mathrel{\equiv_{m}}k
.

Now, we prove that each model
\nu
of
\exists y(\varphi(\vec{x},y)\land y\mathrel{\equiv_{m}}k)
is
also a model of
\varphi(\vec{x},c^{\prime})
. Since
\nu
is a model of the first formula,
we have that there is some
\ell
s.t.
\nu^{\prime}=\nu\cup\{y\mapsto\ell\}
is a model of
\varphi(\vec{x},y)\land y\mathrel{\equiv_{m}}k
.
Since
\varphi
is
c
-best from below w.r.t.
y
, we have that
\ell\leq c
. Secondly,
from the second conjunct we get that
\ell\mathrel{\equiv_{m}}k
. From the definition of
c^{\prime}
we have that
\ell\leq c^{\prime}
.
Finally, if we again use the property of best-from-belowness of
\varphi
together with
\ell\leq c^{\prime}
,
we obtain that
\nu
is a model of
\varphi(\vec{x},c^{\prime})
.
∎

Lemma 8

For
a_{y},a_{m},m,k,c\in\mathbb{Z}
and
M\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, if there is
y_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
a_{y}\cdot y_{1}+a_{m}\cdot m\mathrel{\equiv_{M}}k
, then there
exist
y_{2}\in[c-\alpha+1,c]
and
y_{3}\in[c,c+\alpha-1]
such that

a_{y}\cdot y_{2}+a_{m}\cdot m\mathrel{\equiv_{M}}k
and

a_{y}\cdot y_{3}+a_{m}\cdot m\mathrel{\equiv_{M}}k
for
\alpha=\frac{M}{\gcd(a_{y},M)}
.

Proof

Assume that
a_{y}\cdot y_{1}+a_{m}\cdot m\mathrel{\equiv_{M}}k
and let us show how
to choose
y_{2}
from the interval such that
a_{y}\cdot y_{2}+a_{m}\cdot m\mathrel{\equiv_{M}}k
.
Rewriting the two modulo constraints into equations, we get the following two linear constraints,

\displaystyle a_{y}\cdot y_{1}+a_{m}\cdot m+d_{1}\cdot M

\displaystyle=k

(22)

\displaystyle a_{y}\cdot y_{2}+a_{m}\cdot m+d_{2}\cdot M

\displaystyle=k,

(23)

for some
d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
From equality of the left-hand sides, we obtain

a_{y}\cdot y_{1}+d_{1}\cdot M=a_{y}\cdot y_{2}+d_{2}\cdot M

(24)

and, after rearrangement,

a_{y}\cdot y_{1}-a_{y}\cdot y_{2}=d_{2}\cdot M-d_{1}\cdot M,

(25)

which is equivalent to

a_{y}(y_{1}-y_{2})=M(d_{2}-d_{1}).

(26)

Since
d_{2}
and
d_{1}
are both existentially quantified, we can substitute
d_{2}-d_{1}
by an existentially quanfitied variable
d
, obtaining

a_{y}(y_{1}-y_{2})=M\cdot d.

(27)

Note that since we are reasoning over integers, we cannot just divide the
right-hand side by
a_{y}
.
Instead, let
\beta=\gcd(a_{y},M)
.
Then we can express
a_{y}
and
M
as
a_{y}=a_{y}^{\prime}\cdot\beta
and
M=M^{\prime}\cdot\beta
, so

a_{y}^{\prime}\cdot\beta\cdot(y_{1}-y_{2})=M^{\prime}\cdot\beta\cdot d.

(28)

The
\beta
’s cancel out, so we get

a_{y}^{\prime}(y_{1}-y_{2})=M^{\prime}\cdot d

(29)

where
a_{y}^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}
are coprime.
Because of this, the equation can only hold if
d
is a multiple of
a_{y}^{\prime}
,
i.e.,
d=h\cdot a_{y}^{\prime}
for some
h\in\mathbb{Z}
:

a_{y}^{\prime}(y_{1}-y_{2})=M^{\prime}\cdot h\cdot a_{y}^{\prime}

(30)

Now,
a_{y}^{\prime}
’s cancel out, i.e.,

y_{1}-y_{2}=M^{\prime}\cdot h\qquad\implies\qquad y_{2}=y_{1}-M^{\prime}\cdot h.

(31)

Because
M=M^{\prime}\cdot\gcd(a_{y},M)
, we have that
M^{\prime}=\frac{M}{\gcd(a_{y},M)}
, which is the same as the definition of
\alpha
.
Therefore, for any interval of the size at least
\alpha
, there will be
a value of
h
such that
y_{2}=y_{1}-\alpha\cdot h
is in the interval.
We can duplicate similar reasoning for
y_{3}
.
∎

See
7

Proof

From
Lemma

8
and the fact that
\psi
is

c
-decreasing w.r.t.
y
, it follows that it is sufficient to consider
y
in the interval

[c-\alpha+1,c]
.
Therefore, for linearizing the modulo constraint
a_{y}\cdot y+a_{m}\cdot m\mathrel{\equiv_{M}}k
into a disjunction of linear equations of the form

a_{y}\cdot y+a_{m}\cdot m=k+\ell_{j}\cdot\alpha
for different
\ell_{j}
that
intersect the rectangle
[c-\alpha+1,c]\times[r,s]
, we need to

(a)

find the
\ell_{1}
of the left-most line intersecting the rectangle (we
assume
y
is on the horizontal axis and
m
is on the vertical axis) and

(b)

find the number
N
of lines intersecting the rectangle.

The value of
\ell_{1}
is computed using a technical excursion into high
school analytic geometry that we do not reproduce here (we just note that the
case split is to distinguish lines representing increasing/decreasing
functions).
From
\ell_{1}
, we compute the
y
-coordinate of the left-most line
intersecting the rectangle, denoted as
y_{1}
, and also
N
, both by using
analytic geometry one more time.
∎
